Discussion Name:
Should the US adopt stricter gun control legislation?

1. Thesis:
The US should adopt stricter gun control legislation [1].

  1.1. Pro:
  Stricter gun control legislation may reduce [2] instances of gun-related injury, accidental death, murder, and suicide.

    1.1.1. Pro:
    The presence of a gun in a household greatly increases the risk [3] of suicide (p. 935 [4]), indicated by the fact that more than half [5] of all suicides in the US are carried out with a firearm [6].

      1.1.1.1. Pro:
      Passing a typical background check does not guarantee [7] that an individual would not use their gun to harm themself.

        1.1.1.1.1. Con:
        Background checks that include screening for mental illness [8] are associated with reduced firearm and total suicide rates.

        1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
        Mental health evaluations are not particularly accurate or effective [9] in assessing an individual's suicide risk.

        1.1.1.1.3. Con:
        Background checks on mental health do not need to guarantee that individuals will not commit violence using a gun in order to be useful; preventing those at the highest risk of violence [10] from accessing guns would still be an improvement.

      1.1.1.2. Con:
      There is no apparent connection [11] between suicide and gun ownership rates.

        1.1.1.2.1. Pro:
        A number of countries, such as South Korea, experience high suicide rates [12] despite having exceptionally low rates of gun ownership [13].

          1.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
          Even if the suicide rate is unaffected, South Korea's strict gun regulations have made homicide by gun virtually nonexistent [14].

          1.1.1.2.1.2. Pro:
          Lithuania and Japan have high suicide rates [15] despite low rates of gun ownership.

        1.1.1.2.2. Con:
        Suicide by gun inherently involves access to a gun regardless of ownership. Strict gun controls would limit such access and therefore would reduce the instances of suicide by gun.

        1.1.1.2.3. Pro:
        Individuals with suicide ideation who do not have access to a fiream often find alternative ways [16] to end their lives.

      1.1.1.3. Con:
      A desperately suicidal person could carry out the act through other means.

        1.1.1.3.1. Con:
        Suicide by gun has a 90% fatality rate [17], which is the highest case fatality rate [18] compared to all other methods.

          1.1.1.3.1.1. Con:
          While gun-related suicides are tragic, mental health concerns are best addressed with increased funding and public access to mental health services rather than by legislative action.

          1.1.1.3.1.2. Pro:
          Since nine out of ten people who unsuccessfully attempt suicide do not end up dying by suicide [19] at a later date, stricter gun controls would significantly reduce the number of successfully completed suicides.

        1.1.1.3.2. Pro:
        Suffocation [20] is a common method of committing suicide, and can be enacted with common household items.

        1.1.1.3.3. Con:
        Firearm suicide is a particularly instantaneous form of suicide (p. 3 [21]), so those with access to it have very little time to reconsider or seek medical help.

        1.1.1.3.4. Pro:
        Suicide by self-poisoning or overdose [22] is increasingly common among young people in the US.

      1.1.1.4. Pro:
      Restricting individuals in high-risk groups [23] from accessing firearms reduces violence.

        1.1.1.4.1. Pro:
        Measures introduced to restrict the sale of firearms to individuals with previous case history of domestic violence dramatically reduced incidences of intimate partner violence (p. 31 [24]).

        1.1.1.4.2. Con:
        While restricting those with a history of mental illness [25] has the potential to prevent gun violence, doing so is discriminatory towards such individuals unless they also have a criminal record of violent offenses (p. 3 [26]).

        1.1.1.4.3. Pro:
        Preventing those with a history of certain violent misdemeanors from owning or possessing a gun could reduce homicide rates by up to 19% [27].

        1.1.1.4.4. Con:
        Limiting the rights of individuals based on risk can contribute to discriminatory policies; for example, areas with high gun murder rates [28] could be considered high risk and would, therefore, deserve additional restrictions on all residents.

    1.1.2. Pro:
    Empirical evidence indicates that there is a strong and significant link between weak gun laws and high rates of gun violence [29] and organized crime [30].

      1.1.2.1. Pro:
      Weak gun laws have combined with underinvestment and economic disparity to produce a disproportionately high rate of gun violence [31] in communities of color.

        1.1.2.1.1. Pro:
        Black Americans are 10 times more likely [32] to die by gun homicide than white Americans.

        1.1.2.1.2. Con:
        Gun control laws have historically been used as a method of exerting control over minority communities [33].

      1.1.2.2. Pro:
      Across high-income nations like the US, areas with more firearms tend [34] to have a greater number [35] of homicides.

        1.1.2.2.1. Con:
        Correlation does not imply causality; confounding factors could explain why people who live in areas with a higher homicide rate own more guns.

        1.1.2.2.2. Con:
        The number of guns in circulation has increased steadily in the US for decades, while violent crime rates, including gun crime, have steadily decreased [36].

          1.1.2.2.2.1. Pro:
          Armed citizens often prevent [37] violent crimes and homicides.

          1.1.2.2.2.2. Con:
          This has more to do with the overall general decrease [38] in violent crime in the US.

          1.1.2.2.2.3. Con:
          Evidence for the correlation between a decrease in violent crime and increased firearm ownership is mixed [39]; most researchers have found a lack of support [40] for such a connection.

        1.1.2.2.3. Pro:
        Access to a gun strongly increases [41] the likelihood of dying by murder.

          1.1.2.2.3.1. Con:
          The Reuters source [42] is problematic because it uses studies published between 1988 and 2005. Since then, homicide rates have declined significantly, while the number of guns has increased [43].

        1.1.2.2.4. Pro:
        Cross-analyzing data on firearm incidents with the number of firearm regulations [44] shows that increasing [45] regulations does decrease [46] firearm deaths and rates of violent crime [47].

          1.1.2.2.4.1. Pro:
          In 2007, Missouri repealed its handgun permit law and immediately saw an increase in firearm murders, but there was no increase in other types of murders (p. 2 [48]).

          1.1.2.2.4.2. Con:
          Looking at statewide rather than citywide [49] statistics misrepresents the data by failing to acknowledge the correlation between large cities and homicides irrespective of gun ownership in the state.

      1.1.2.3. Con:
      Gun control does not prevent gun violence.

        1.1.2.3.1. Con:
        The presence of a firearm is strongly associated with increased feelings of aggression and leads to more violent, controlling, and aggressive [50] behavior (p. 359 [51]).

          1.1.2.3.1.1. Pro:
          An increase in aggression has been correlated [52] with an increase in crime.

          1.1.2.3.1.2. Con:
          Evidence suggests that the link between the presence of a firearm and aggression, known as the weapons effect, is weak or nonexistent (pp. 59–60 [53]).

        1.1.2.3.2. Pro:
        Stricter firearm ownership laws will not effectively remove firearms from the possession of criminals [54], who, by definition, operate outside the law [55].

        1.1.2.3.3. Pro:
        Gun violence is a symptom of economic inequality [56] and declining trust [57] in US institutions (pp. 1–2 [58]). The US needs to overcome such divisions before considering further gun regulations.

          1.1.2.3.3.1. Con:
          The US federal government has a discretionary spending budget of US$1.485 trillion [59], which it could earmark for both tackling access to guns and inequality if it chooses.

          1.1.2.3.3.2. Pro:
          Of all the countries with a higher rate of inequality than the US according to the Gini index [60], only Costa Rica and South Africa had lower rates of gun homicide [61] per annum.

          1.1.2.3.3.3. Con:
          Any attempts to address socioeconomic issues are unlikely to be effective without first addressing the high rates of violence in urban communities; unsafe communities are unable to prosper [62].

        1.1.2.3.4. Con:
        The Dickey [63] and Tiahrt [64] Amendments have made it nearly [65] impossible [66] for the government to sufficiently research or analyze gun violence statistics to support or refute this point.

          1.1.2.3.4.1. Pro:
          The Tiahrt Amendments [67] undermine the collection of data pertaining to gun sales by requiring the destruction or witholding of all approved gun purchaser records.

          1.1.2.3.4.2. Con:
          The Tiahrt Amendments [68] only protect the data of legal firearm sales and prevent the analysis of data unrelated to criminal activity.

          1.1.2.3.4.3. Con:
          Despite the Dickey [69] Amendment, gun violence research in recent years has actually increased, since the US government has continued to fund [70] it through the NIH [71].

      1.1.2.4. Con:
      Prohibitions [72] generally don't harm [73] organized crime, and instead fuel the creation of illegal trade to fill the needs of the market (p. 1 [74]).

        1.1.2.4.1. Pro:
        Guns sold on the black market are likely to give higher profits [75] to the criminals, gangs, cartels, and mafia who traffic these products.

        1.1.2.4.2. Con:
        New gun control legislation could create stiffer penalties for illegal gun sales and trafficking, increasing the risk for such organizations (pp. 1–2 [76]).

    1.1.3. Con:
    Given the existing lack of enforcement [77], adopting stricter gun controls is unlikely to save that many more lives.

      1.1.3.1. Con:
      Rejecting gun control legislation as a partial solution because it is not a perfect solution is an example of the Nirvana fallacy [78].

        1.1.3.1.1. Con:
        The effectiveness of gun control legislation is not a black-and-white issue; it is reasonable to reject measures that are likely to be ineffective or pose significant new issues.

        1.1.3.1.2. Pro:
        Laws and regulations rarely solve problems completely, but that doesn't mean that they serve no purpose. For example, even though methamphetamine is still being manufactured, drug laws [79] have made it more difficult to acquire the necessary chemicals.

      1.1.3.2. Pro:
      Individual citizens should hold their elected representatives accountable for the enforcement of current laws [80] rather than creating more ineffective laws.

        1.1.3.2.1. Con:
        Effective enforcement of existing gun control measures is often hindered by a lack of resources [81]; new legislation could designate funds for this purpose.

        1.1.3.2.2. Pro:
        There are poor rates of prosecution [82] and indictment [83] of felons who break the law by being in possession of a firearm. Without enforcing the existing legislation and studying the effects, adding restrictions to law-abiding gun owners makes little sense.

    1.1.4. Con:
    In the US, it is legal to use lethal force in self-defense [84] if one's life is threatened. Gun control legislation limits an individual's capacity for self-defense.

      1.1.4.1. Con:
      There are safer, cheaper, and more effective methods of self-defense than using firearms.

        1.1.4.1.1. Con:
        Guns are an effective last resort [85] to ensuring survival while trying to defend oneself.

          1.1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
          Discharging a firearm is an effective and reliable last resort for self-defense if it is justified by the fervent belief [86] that someone is likely to die or be seriously injured otherwise.

        1.1.4.1.2. Pro:
        Tasers [87], locks, and other security systems [88] are effective crime deterrents and do not carry the risks [89] associated with keeping a firearm for defense.

          1.1.4.1.2.1. Pro:
          In a self-defense scenario, there is always a possibility of being disarmed [90] and having one's own firearm used against them.

          1.1.4.1.2.2. Con:
          Tasers do not always work [91].

        1.1.4.1.3. Con:
        Guns allow people to defend themselves effectively [92] from a distance and remove the physical advantages of an attacker.

        1.1.4.1.4. Pro:
        In most cases [93], there is no evidence that having a gun aids in self-defense.

          1.1.4.1.4.1. Con:
          Historically, criminals have claimed that they were deterred from committing certain crimes due to the fear of their victim being armed (p. 1 [94]).

          1.1.4.1.4.2. Pro:
          Victims carrying a firearm for self-defense are often unable to protect themselves from, and are more likely than an unarmed victim to be shot during, an assault (pp. 2034, 2037 [95]).

          1.1.4.1.4.3. Pro:
          The adage [96] "the only way to stop a bad guy with a gun, is a good guy with a gun" has been proven false as having a gun does not aid in being able to defend oneself [97] during a mass shooting.

            1.1.4.1.4.3.1. Pro:
            People carrying guns have openly admitted not drawing them during mass shootings out of fear of being mistakenly targeted [98] by police or other bystanders upon their arrival at the scene.

            1.1.4.1.4.3.2. Con:
            Police generally respond quickly [99] to mass shootings; several have been stopped within minutes of their arrival, demonstrating that mass shootings are best controlled by professionals.

            1.1.4.1.4.3.3. Pro:
            Some mass shooters, such as the Las Vegas shooter [100], carry out attacks with long-range weapons from advantageous positions [101]. In such a scenario, victims carrying weapons are unable to fire back without risking the safety of themselves or others.

        1.1.4.1.5. Pro:
        Firearms training is expensive and optional, so average gun-owners are unlikely to be trained as well as police officers, for whom a minimum level of competency [102] is mandatory. Citizen defense could, therefore, lead to further complications.

          1.1.4.1.5.1. Pro:
          Restricting gun ownership would protect police officers and their families, given that shootings account for 5 out of 6 officer deaths [103].

          1.1.4.1.5.2. Pro:
          Most civilians do not have advanced de-escalation [104] or crisis training and are far more likely to exacerbate a bad situation by making misjudgments in the stress of a crisis.

          1.1.4.1.5.3. Con:
          Since the majority of police officers have never had to fire their weapon [105], in many places gun enthusiasts are equally or more [106] skilled with their firearms than police officers [107].

            1.1.4.1.5.3.1. Con:
            During major NRA events, which attract many legal gun owners from around the country, the rate of gun injuries declines by 20% [108], which implies that training and experience are insufficient for improving gun safety.

          1.1.4.1.5.4. Pro:
          Police are required to pass sidearms training several times per year [109].

          1.1.4.1.5.5. Con:
          There have been several cases of armed civilians [110] limiting mass [111] shootings by wounding or killing the assailant.

      1.1.4.2. Pro:
      The incidence of home invasion robberies is on the rise in the US [112]. Gun owners can effectively stop those who attempt to harm their family or loved ones.

        1.1.4.2.1. Con:
        The number of robberies in the US has declined [113] in recent years.

        1.1.4.2.2. Pro:
        In 2016 [114], there was a 3.4% rise in the violent crime rate in the US, the largest single-year gain in 25 years.

      1.1.4.3. Con:
      Guns are rarely [115] used in legitimate self-defense compared with how often they are used to commit crimes.

        1.1.4.3.1. Pro:
        An FBI study of 160 active shooter incidents [116] found that only seven of them were stopped by an armed civilian, and six of those seven were armed security guards or off-duty police officers.

        1.1.4.3.2. Pro:
        For every time a gun kept in the home is used in self-defense, there are four accidental shootings, seven assaults or homicides, and eleven attempted or completed suicides (p. 1 [117]).

          1.1.4.3.2.1. Con:
          This only includes occasions when a firearm was used defensively to injure or kill an assailant. In reality, in around three quarters of all defensive gun use cases firearms acted as a sufficient deterrent [118].

        1.1.4.3.3. Con:
        Various polls collected from 1989 to 1994 indicate that defensive gun use (DGU) data is underreported and varies wildly [119] in different years and states (pp. 182–187 [120]).

          1.1.4.3.3.1. Pro:
          The defensive use of guns (DGUs) by crime victims is estimated to be at least as common [121] as offensive uses by criminals.

            1.1.4.3.3.1.1. Pro:
            The study estimated that defensive uses of guns annually could range from 500,000 to 3 million [122].

            1.1.4.3.3.1.2. Con:
            The article points out that the number of defensive gun uses (DGUs) each year remains unknown [123], so it is speculative to claim that DGUs are as common as offensive gun uses.

            1.1.4.3.3.1.3. Con:
            The article misquotes [124] the CDC in order to discredit gun use data, claiming that the CDC's goal is to paint gun use as “dirty, deadly — and banned [125]," when in fact this quote was about tobacco.

          1.1.4.3.3.2. Con:
          From 2007 to 2011, only 0.1% of victims of nonviolent crimes used a gun defensively during the attack (p. 12 [126]).

        1.1.4.3.4. Pro:
        The ratio of justifiable [127] killings to murders [128], suicides, or accidental gun-related deaths was 1 to 32 in 2012, which indicates that most gun-related deaths were not the result of self-defense.

        1.1.4.3.5. Con:
        The surveys used to estimate the number of guns used defensively are flawed because they leave out instances in which a gun is used to deter a potential threat [129].

      1.1.4.4. Con:
      There is a fine line between self-defense and the illegal use of force. The difference between the two might be difficult to judge in the heat of the moment.

        1.1.4.4.1. Con:
        The US criminal justice system differentiates [130] between self-defense and criminal acts by assessing the presence of an imminent threat, reasonable fear, and the use of proportional force.

          1.1.4.4.1.1. Pro:
          A threat is imminent if it is ongoing, meaning that any act taken after a threat has resolved is considered an unlawful act of retribution [131].

          1.1.4.4.1.2. Con:
          Self-defense laws are interpreted differently [132] from case to case.

        1.1.4.4.2. Pro:
        Stand your ground [133] and self defense laws can be abused by bad-faith attackers in the case of fatal shootings, as the other person cannot share their perspective.

          1.1.4.4.2.1. Pro:
          Expansive self-defense laws, such as stand your ground laws, often perpetuate racial bias [134] against vulnerable minority communities (p. 10 [135]).

          1.1.4.4.2.2. Con:
          Stand your ground laws have been shown to deter crime in some places, such as Oklahoma [136] and Texas [137].

            1.1.4.4.2.2.1. Con:
            The reduction in crime in Houston is attributed to the controversial use of the Castle Doctrine to justify the Joe Horn shooting [138]. The crime rates in Dallas [139], conversely, remained unaffected after its introduction.

            1.1.4.4.2.2.2. Pro:
            In 2012, a woman in Oklahoma [140] was given permission by the police dispatcher to use deadly force to defend her family from an oncoming home intruder.

          1.1.4.4.2.3. Pro:
          There is evidence [141] that stand your ground [142] laws empower individuals [143] to use violence, making shootings more likely.

        1.1.4.4.3. Pro:
        The right to self-defense is limited. For example, in some cases, the victim of an assault has a duty to retreat [144] and is not entitled to the right to self-defense.

      1.1.4.5. Pro:
      The right to self-defense is only effective if it's a right to proportional self-defense. Thus, if a person is being threatened with a gun, they should be able to defend themself with a gun.

        1.1.4.5.1. Pro:
        The imminent threat of being attacked with lethal force is sufficient cause for preemptively using lethal force in self-defense (p. 1185–1186 [145]).

        1.1.4.5.2. Con:
        People only have rights to things they need for survival, not things they merely want.

          1.1.4.5.2.1. Con:
          A number of enshrined human rights are required conditions for human development and fulfillment rather than survival needs.

          1.1.4.5.2.2. Con:
          Firearm ownership allows people to protect their right to life, exercise liberty, and feel safe enough to live a happy life.

          1.1.4.5.2.3. Pro:
          Unalienable rights [146] are rights to things like clean food and water, safe shelter, and free thought.

    1.1.5. Pro:
    Educating [147] the public on the facts of responsible gun handling and ownership is paramount to firearm safety in a gun-legal society.

      1.1.5.1. Con:
      Safe storage laws are unconstitutional [148] and unenforceable [149] without random home searches by police.

        1.1.5.1.1. Pro:
        Safe storage laws fail [150] to specifically stipulate the nature of the firearm storage needed.

        1.1.5.1.2. Con:
        A number of courts across the US have ruled that safe storage laws are not unconstitutional [151].

      1.1.5.2. Con:
      Many gun owners take gun ownership seriously enough to voluntarily take a pledge [152] to educate their children about guns and properly store and secure their weapons.

        1.1.5.2.1. Con:
        Pledges are not enforceable [153] and are easily forgotten.

        1.1.5.2.2. Pro:
        Project Childsafe has also created a pledge for children [154] to promote awareness of safe gun practices.

      1.1.5.3. Pro:
      Gun control laws in California have demonstrated [155] a strong correlation with the prevention of accidental gun deaths, which is likely attributable to law-abiding gun owners acting in safer ways with firearms.

        1.1.5.3.1. Pro:
        Recent California legislation has included a requirement that gun owners carry liability insurance, with financial incentives [156] for enacting common safety measures.

        1.1.5.3.2. Con:
        The annual number of gun-related accidental deaths has declined [157] by more than 66% in the last 25 years, irrespective of changes to legislation.

      1.1.5.4. Pro:
      Requiring gun owners to pass a state-administered test would raise their competency [158] in the storage, maintenance, and operation of their firearms, thereby reducing the number of accidental shootings.

        1.1.5.4.1. Pro:
        Waiting periods create a cooling-off period among buyers and significantly reduce [159] the incidence of gun violence in the US (p. 12162 [160]).

          1.1.5.4.1.1. Pro:
          Requiring people to apply for a license would prevent them from impulsively [161] buying a gun in a fit of anger. This could reduce the incidence of crimes of passion [162].

          1.1.5.4.1.2. Con:
          Gun purchase waiting times have been linked with reduced suicide [163], but evidence for the effect of waiting periods on violent crime and intimate partner homicide is inconclusive [164].

        1.1.5.4.2. Con:
        In states where mandatory training courses are required before a gun license is issued, the courses often overlook [165] major safety components.

        1.1.5.4.3. Pro:
        Stricter regulation would make people consider their decision to own a gun more seriously.

  1.2. Con:
  Open access to guns is a cultural characteristic of the US and thus should be preserved.

    1.2.1. Pro:
    Gun ownership is a key cultural element in US subculture and is imbued with symbolic significance for much of American society (p. 7 [166]).

      1.2.1.1. Con:
      Gun culture [167] has a number of harmful consequences that would be mitigated by stricter gun control.

        1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
        US gun culture is inextricably linked to toxic masculinity [168].

        1.2.1.1.2. Con:
        US gun culture promotes positive civic values [169].

        1.2.1.1.3. Pro:
        US gun culture endangers children [170] by placing them in environments with unregulated and insecure firearms.

          1.2.1.1.3.1. Pro:
          Due to the lack of state restrictions [171] on gun safety in the home, children [172] under the age of 18 often have easy access to a firearm [173] at home.

            1.2.1.1.3.1.1. Pro:
            More than 80% of children who die by suicide [174] with a firearm used a gun owned by a family member.

            1.2.1.1.3.1.2. Pro:
            The majority of the firearms used in school shootings [175] in the 1990s were obtained either from the shooters' own homes, or from friends or relatives.

            1.2.1.1.3.1.3. Con:
            Laws do not necessarily change human behavior [176]; people who are irresponsible enough to allow children [177] to gain access to firearms are unlikely to behave more responsibly simply because of a new law.

            1.2.1.1.3.1.4. Con:
            Specialized gun safes [178] can simultaneously store weapons properly and offer quick access for home defense.

              1.2.1.1.3.1.4.1. Con:
              Even the most advanced gun safes commonly rely upon a mechanism with known flaws [179], which has made weapons accessible to children as young as three years old [180].

          1.2.1.1.3.2. Con:
          The Child Safety Lock Act of 2005 [181] mandated that all non-private handguns must be locked up and come with gun-safety instructions, yet this has failed to keep children safe.

        1.2.1.1.4. Con:
        Groups like the NRA are strong proponents of gun culture and advocate for gun safety [182].

          1.2.1.1.4.1. Con:
          The NRA desires for guns to be available in every aspect of society and is adamantly opposed [183] to the creation of gun-free zones, which are less frequently the targets [184] of mass shooters.

          1.2.1.1.4.2. Con:
          The NRA supports nationwide Constitutional Carry legislation [185], which allows anyone over the age of 18 to purchase a gun without any prior training or permits required.

          1.2.1.1.4.3. Pro:
          Texas lawmakers earmarked US$1 million for a safe gun storage campaign in the state; the NRA was unopposed to the budget line item [186].

          1.2.1.1.4.4. Con:
          The NRA's Eddie Eagle GunSafe program, which purports to teach kids gun safety, has been demonstrated to be ineffective [187], falling short of instituting behavioral change that would help save lives.

            1.2.1.1.4.4.1. Pro:
            The Eddie Eagle GunSafe program was created by the NRA in the 1980s to convince state legislatures that enacting laws to protect children wasn't necessary [188]. It was never meant to be a legitimate safety program for children.

          1.2.1.1.4.5. Pro:
          The NRA offers nationwide training and certification programs, including home firearm safety [189] courses.

            1.2.1.1.4.5.1. Con:
            The NRA has lobbied against [190] requiring gun safety training for gun owners in states which have proposed such laws.

      1.2.1.2. Con:
      Rejecting gun control legislation in order to preserve past cultural norms commits the appeal to tradition [191] (argumentum ad antiquitatem) fallacy.

        1.2.1.2.1. Con:
        Traditions often embody important cultural values and play an integral role in intergenerational cultural transmission (p. 1 [192]).

        1.2.1.2.2. Pro:
        A practice being a traditional element of a specific culture does not mean that it should be preserved when it is demonstrably harmful.

          1.2.1.2.2.1. Pro:
          A number of traditional cultural practices, such as childhood marriage or female genital mutilation [193], are harmful and discouraged by modern societies.

          1.2.1.2.2.2. Con:
          There is a lower incidence of injuries in shooting sports than in other sports (pp. 11–12 [194]).

            1.2.1.2.2.2.1. Con:
            The overall incidence of injury might be lower but the severity and potential for fatal injuries must be accounted for through heavy regulation and strict procedures (p. 1 [195]).

      1.2.1.3. Pro:
      The most commonly cited reason for gun ownership in the US is recreation (pp. 2–3 [196]).

        1.2.1.3.1. Con:
        The majority of gun owners do not use their guns regularly [197], and many of them only own the gun for self-defense purposes.

        1.2.1.3.2. Pro:
        More than 10 million Americans each year use guns for hunting [198]—in Alaska, gun ownership is predominantly for hunting purposes [199].

        1.2.1.3.3. Pro:
        More than 32 million Americans use their guns for target shooting [200].

          1.2.1.3.3.1. Con:
          Shooting ranges have strict controls regarding the proper storage and handling of weapons, and the types of weapons and ammunition [201] permitted; future legislation could mirror these restrictions to protect the public at large.

          1.2.1.3.3.2. Pro:
          Shooting sports are common around the world and currently represent 15 Olympic events [202].

        1.2.1.3.4. Con:
        People would likely be able to continue to visit shooting ranges and gun clubs even with stricter gun control legislation; such places would likely continue to exist as they are protected and regulated by statutes in every US state (pp. 7–100 [203]).

      1.2.1.4. Pro:
      Sporting subculture in the US is intergenerational and linked to Southern identity; it is strongly associated with American individualism (p. 233 [204]).

        1.2.1.4.1. Con:
        Sporting culture in the Southern US intersects with honor culture, which is associated with increased gun ownership, aggressive masculinity, and higher rates of violence (p. 958 [205]).

        1.2.1.4.2. Pro:
        Individualism [206] is a core American value; gun control laws represent collectivism and are thus incompatible with many American traditions (pp. 229–230 [207]).

      1.2.1.5. Con:
      The change in behavior generated by the implementation of tightened gun controls will change cultural norms about gun possession, leading people to consider guns less vital to their culture.

        1.2.1.5.1. Pro:
        Government actions change the way people behave and respond to things. Even though the chance of a terrorist attack is much lower than gun violence, many Americans fear terrorist attacks more [208] because of the legislation surrounding both.

        1.2.1.5.2. Con:
        Creating more gun regulations will not change cultural perceptions around guns. Policy is generally downstream of culture.

          1.2.1.5.2.1. Pro:
          The culture surrounding marijuana use in the US did not yield to strict regulations. Given the prevalence of marijuana use in the US in spite of the risk of legal consequences, many states [209] have legalized its use.

        1.2.1.5.3. Con:
        Given that the congressional approval rating is often below 25% [210] by most polls, gun control could easily signal that the government is attempting to erode major constitutional rights for their own benefit.

          1.2.1.5.3.1. Con:
          The approval rating of the US Congress has not reached 30% in 10 years [211], which suggests that there is no reason to assume that there would be an adverse response to gun control legislation.

        1.2.1.5.4. Pro:
        Owning a gun is often a taboo [212] in places [213] where firearms are only seen in the hands of military, police, security agents, or hunters.

          1.2.1.5.4.1. Pro:
          Reducing the availability of something reduces its social and cultural relevance. The social shift in acceptance of smoking is a comparable example, where legal restrictions cut down its prevalence (p. 1 [214]).

          1.2.1.5.4.2. Con:
          Making gun ownership a taboo increases the mystique and personal power associated with the transgression of gun ownership laws (p. 19 [215]).

    1.2.2. Con:
    Polls suggest most American citizens support [216] strict gun control legislation that is enforced fairly.

      1.2.2.1. Con:
      Polls also suggest that most American citizens support [217] the use of guns for self-defense.

        1.2.2.1.1. Pro:
        Placing reasonable restrictions on gun purchases and ownership supports the concept of self-defense by keeping guns out of the hands of those more likely to use them offensively [218].

        1.2.2.1.2. Con:
        Any restriction on the right to carry a gun detracts from the ability of individuals to defend themselves (p. 1040 [219]).

      1.2.2.2. Pro:
      It is a misconception that gun owners are opposed to stricter regulation; there is evidence that the majority of gun owners favor additional restrictions [220].

        1.2.2.2.1. Pro:
        Even among those who favor gun control legislation, there is little public interest in banning the use of guns for recreational purposes (p. 38 [221]).

        1.2.2.2.2. Con:
        A number of rural counties remain staunchly opposed to any gun control legislation [222] and have declared themselves sanctuaries for the ownership of forbidden guns.

      1.2.2.3. Con:
      Appealing to popularity [223] is logically problematic.

        1.2.2.3.1. Pro:
        Historically, some quite popular laws [224] were later deemed immoral.

        1.2.2.3.2. Con:
        Democratic laws should reflect the will [225] of the majority.

        1.2.2.3.3. Con:
        Making the hasty generalization [226] that gun control is wrong in order to avoid appealing to popularity is also logically problematic.

      1.2.2.4. Pro:
      About 75% of US adults and 60% of gun owners support [227] laws that require prospective buyers to get a license from law enforcement (p. 880 [228]).

        1.2.2.4.1. Pro:
        A gun licensing system [229], like the one employed in Massachusetts, could limit the number of guns in circulation by preventing people from building up a cache of weapons.

          1.2.2.4.1.1. Con:
          Gun sales in Massachusetts have hit record levels [230] in recent years, indicating that such restrictions are ineffective at reducing overall gun ownership.

          1.2.2.4.1.2. Pro:
          By requiring potential gun owners to jump through many hoops [231] to obtain their license, such systems ensure purchases are made rationally, necessarily, and without ill intent.

    1.2.3. Con:
    Free, unregulated access to guns has never been a part of the American way.

      1.2.3.1. Pro:
      In the American Old West [232] (roughly 1850–1910), there were a number of strict gun laws [233], including laws against openly carrying weapons in certain towns and requiring the registration of firearms with the sheriff.

        1.2.3.1.1. Con:
        Such laws in the Old West were selectively enforced [234] and only applied to specific areas and persons.

        1.2.3.1.2. Pro:
        The “Conductor Generalis,” used by sheriffs, constables, and justices of the peace during colonial and revolutionary times, laid out the conditions for disarming citizens (pp. 117–118 [235]) including, but not limited to, the action of affray (p. vi [236]).

      1.2.3.2. Pro:
      The US has passed several restrictions on the purchase, sale, and use of firearms over the last century [237].

        1.2.3.2.1. Con:
        A number of US Supreme Court cases [238] have reaffirmed the right of Americans to bear arms.

        1.2.3.2.2. Pro:
        Some states have laws which dictate how guns should be stored [239] and at what age someone is eligible [240] to buy and carry a firearm.

      1.2.3.3. Con:
      Free access does not necessarily equate to unlimited access. By the same logic that requiring a license to publicly carry a firearm is acceptable, owning an unlicensed firearm on private property should also be permitted.

        1.2.3.3.1. Con:
        Requiring a license at home is a reasonable way to protect children and visitors from being fatally injured by a weapon.

        1.2.3.3.2. Pro:
        For example, in the US it is generally legal to drive a vehicle without a license on private property [241], but to do so on a public highway requires a license.

    1.2.4. Pro:
    Strict gun control policies would significantly harm weapons manufacturers and vendors.

      1.2.4.1. Con:
      Placing more value on the profits of the gun industry [242] than on the importance of the lives lost due to gun violence is highly immoral.

        1.2.4.1.1. Pro:
        Everyone has the right to life, while guns empower people to threaten the lives of others. The right to life [243] is more important than the right to own a gun.

      1.2.4.2. Pro:
      Decreasing the demand for guns could lead to massive layoffs in the firearm and ammunition industry, which employs [244] a significant number of Americans.

        1.2.4.2.1. Pro:
        Companies in the US [245] that manufacture, distribute, and sell firearms, ammunition, and hunting equipment directly employ as many as 149,146 people and generate an additional 162,845 jobs in supplier and ancillary industries.

          1.2.4.2.1.1. Con:
          The current US population is 329 million [246], so 300,000 jobs constitutes approximately 0.1% of the population.

        1.2.4.2.2. Con:
        The potential reduction in the number of gun related deaths [247] in the US outweighs the potential layoffs caused by stricter gun regulations.

        1.2.4.2.3. Con:
        Law enforcement and military purchases still account for 40% of revenue [248] for domestic arms manufacturers in the US.

          1.2.4.2.3.1. Con:
          The US civilian population owns an estimated 400 million [249] firearms—more than three times [250] the number of guns held by its military and law enforcement.

      1.2.4.3. Con:
      There would be other economic opportunities for gun manufacturers.

        1.2.4.3.1. Con:
        Even if opportunities existed in the long term, in the short term there would be economic harm to gun manufacturers.

          1.2.4.3.1.1. Con:
          The US is well-known for protecting business interests [251]; the government would certainly introduce economic policies to reduce some of the burden through tax breaks or subsidies.

        1.2.4.3.2. Con:
        Any shift in economic focus would likely require gun manufacturers to retrain their staff as well as discard or invest in new manufacturing equipment, all of which come at a significant cost.

        1.2.4.3.3. Pro:
        Gun manufacturers could pivot to producing other defensive products, such as tasers [252] and Salt guns [253].

        1.2.4.3.4. Pro:
        Local manufacturers could still export their guns to foreign countries. Stricter gun laws in the US do not affect the demand for guns abroad.

    1.2.5. Con:
    Gun laws in the US impact many non-US citizens and countries around the world.

      1.2.5.1. Con:
      The US government has recently established that the needs of the American people should always come first [254], which suggests that the impacts of US laws on other countries and peoples are not valued in US foreign policy.

        1.2.5.1.1. Pro:
        Stephen Moore [255] argues that the most recent "America First" trend in right-wing foreign politics is crucial because the money that the US government spends on aiding other countries is wasted.

        1.2.5.1.2. Con:
        American people should not fear for their lives and those of their children [256]; the best way to place American lives first [257] is through sensible gun control.

      1.2.5.2. Con:
      The US has no obligation to help non-US citizens or resolve problems in other countries.

        1.2.5.2.1. Con:
        The US bears moral responsibility for the downstream effects of its lax gun policies [258], which enable violence around the world.

        1.2.5.2.2. Pro:
        Other countries will unfailingly continue to seek firearms as part of their militarization strategies [259]. The US would be remiss to not take advantage of these financial opportunities.

      1.2.5.3. Pro:
      The Trump administration rolled back a number of restrictions [260], making it easier for gun manufacturers to compete overseas, which could lead to more deaths around the world.

        1.2.5.3.1. Pro:
        Many guns purchased on the black market [261] in Europe come from the US, where sellers exploit weak gun control legislation to purchase guns and ship them overseas.

        1.2.5.3.2. Pro:
        Rather than relaxing the laws, stricter gun control in the US could influence other countries to follow suit, as the US wields a great deal of influence [262] on international public and social policy.

        1.2.5.3.3. Con:
        American arms manufacturers supply approximately one-third [263] of global firearm exports; the firearm industry is important to the American economy.

      1.2.5.4. Pro:
      There are nearly 77 million annual visitors [264] to the US who are legally barred [265] from carrying guns and could be victims of gun violence without any way to defend themselves.

        1.2.5.4.1. Con:
        While many international news outlets [266] claim that gun violence is a threat to tourists visiting the US, it remains safe [267] to visit the US.

        1.2.5.4.2. Pro:
        A number of foreign countries have issued warnings to their citizens [268] about the dangers posed by guns for tourists in the US.

      1.2.5.5. Pro:
      Guns are regularly purchased in the US and then illegally shipped to gangs in both Latin America [269] and the Caribbean [270].

        1.2.5.5.1. Con:
        Many Latin American gangs are able to illegally acquire arms [271] from other sources, including the military and other security services.

        1.2.5.5.2. Pro:
        In a 2015 report [272], the Mexican government estimated that of the 15 million firearms in circulation, 13 million were illegally obtained from the US and are responsible for nearly all gun violence [273] in Mexico.

  1.3. Con:
  Gun control policies infringe on a number of rights that the US government was explicitly founded upon and has an obligation to uphold.

    1.3.1. Con:
    Stricter gun control does not outright violate the rights of citizens to own a firearm. It puts reasonable boundaries [274] around them.

      1.3.1.1. Con:
      The proposed laws would nonetheless limit the firearms available to even a law-abiding, responsible person.

        1.3.1.1.1. Pro:
        People flying to New York City have been arrested for carrying firearms on their journey [275], even when strictly adhering to TSA and federal guidelines.

        1.3.1.1.2. Con:
        The gun control regulations [276] being considered simply expand the current gun laws [277] and thus do not infringe upon the basic rights of gun ownership.

          1.3.1.1.2.1. Con:
          in DC v. Heller [278], the Supreme Court ruled that self-defense is a justifiable reason for gun ownership and that trigger lock requirements are an unreasonable impediment to self-defense. As such, the proposed regulations would constitute infringements.

        1.3.1.1.3. Pro:
        Waiting periods [279] and magazine capacity bans [280] inhibit citizens from protecting themselves.

      1.3.1.2. Pro:
      While access to firearms as tools for self-defense is a fundamental right in the US, it doesn't follow that any and all tools should be freely available for this purpose. It is therefore reasonable to limit the legal access in order to address growing public safety concerns.

        1.3.1.2.1. Con:
        Lethality is an inherent property of firearms, so it does not follow that "more lethal" weapons should be subject to additional restrictions (p. 202 [281]).

        1.3.1.2.2. Pro:
        Just as the right of the people to peaceably assemble [282] can be regulated (via permit), guns should continue to be regulated (via permit) for public safety purposes (p. 5 [283]).

    1.3.2. Pro:
    Gun control legislation effectively violates the due process clause of the Fifth Amendment [284] of the US Constitution by infringing on the gun rights of millions of Americans without any of them first being accused, tried, or convicted of a crime.

      1.3.2.1. Con:
      The lack of strict, enforceable gun regulations undermines the rights and liberties of citizens, who must live in fear of being shot or killed in public.

        1.3.2.1.1. Pro:
        Life, liberty, and security of person are universal human rights [285], which are undermined by inadequate gun control policies.

        1.3.2.1.2. Con:
        Safety is not a right, since that would imply that the government and other citizens bear responsibility for ensuring individual safety.

      1.3.2.2. Pro:
      The Second Amendment right to bear arms represents one of the liberties [286] protected by the Fifth Amendment.

        1.3.2.2.1. Pro:
        In District of Columbia v. Heller, the US Supreme Court confirmed the interpretation of the Second Amendment right to bear arms as a fundamental civil right [287].

        1.3.2.2.2. Con:
        Many of the proposed firearm regulations do not present civil liberty concerns [288].

      1.3.2.3. Pro:
      The Fifth Amendment [289] of the Constitution requires that due process must occur before the state may take away someone's rights due to criminal activity.

        1.3.2.3.1. Pro:
        If the state wishes to deprive someone of a firearm, the state must follow due process in order to link the gun or person to a criminal act.

        1.3.2.3.2. Pro:
        The Gun Control Act of 1968 allows for the removal of gun ownership rights from those convicted of a felony [290] via due process in a state or federal court.

        1.3.2.3.3. Con:
        The Fifth Amendment refers to criminal proceedings [291] and thus is only relevant for protecting due process in the confiscation of guns in criminal cases rather than broader gun control policy.

        1.3.2.3.4. Con:
        Eminent domain [292] allows the US government to take private property to serve a compelling government interest as long as it provides appropriate compensation.

          1.3.2.3.4.1. Pro:
          In extreme cases, private property associated with criminal activity can be seized through civil asset forfeiture [293].

          1.3.2.3.4.2. Con:
          Eminent domain laws, like gun control laws, are unjust because they undermine the constitutional rights of US citizens.

          1.3.2.3.4.3. Con:
          Eminent domain is limited [294] and restricted by due process.

    1.3.3. Pro:
    The US Founding Fathers [295] believed that the right to own a firearm was a necessary responsibility to resist tyranny, which is why it was codified in the Second Amendment [296].

      1.3.3.1. Con:
      There are legal arguments that the Second Amendment does not guarantee the individual right to bear arms, but does allow for "well regulated militia[s] [297]."

        1.3.3.1.1. Pro:
        The Second Amendment only came to represent the ability for any individual to own a firearm after a 2008 Supreme Court ruling [298].

        1.3.3.1.2. Pro:
        Analysis has shown that the Founding Fathers used the phrase "keep and bear arms [299]" expressly in reference to military uses of weapons.

        1.3.3.1.3. Con:
        The modern existence of a standing military [300] rather than reliance on a citizen militia makes private gun ownership more essential as a check on government power [301].

          1.3.3.1.3.1. Pro:
          As the government still controls the military [302], individuals feel that heavily armed civilian militias are a more reliable option, evidenced by the fact that the number and popularity of militias continue to grow [303] in the US.

          1.3.3.1.3.2. Con:
          Given the capacity, scale, and technology of the US military [304], civilian militias are incapable of checking the government's power.

        1.3.3.1.4. Con:
        If private citizens are unable to bear arms, then they are incapable of forming a militia (pp. 86–87 [305]).

      1.3.3.2. Con:
      The Second Amendment [306] was originally written to allow state militias to maintain arms and was included so that states under British rule were more likely to sign on to the new federal government.

        1.3.3.2.1. Con:
        The US Supreme Court has interpreted the Second Amendment as extending the right to own a gun for other purposes apart from militia service [307].

        1.3.3.2.2. Con:
        Even if this were the case, the interpretation of constitutional amendments shifts over time; the original intent of the Second Amendment has little bearing on its modern meaning.

        1.3.3.2.3. Pro:
        The Second Amendment [308] was created when the US was dependent on an armed citizenry for national defense [309], but in the modern day, that role is filled by specialized institutions, such as the police and military.

        1.3.3.2.4. Pro:
        The Second Amendment right to form a militia was not extended to private citizens [310] until two rulings by the Supreme Court as late as 2010.

      1.3.3.3. Pro:
      Gun control legislation represents an infringement [311] on the right to keep and bear arms, which is expressly forbidden by the Second Amendment.

        1.3.3.3.1. Con:
        The US Supreme Court has ruled that placing reasonable restrictions on the right to keep and bear arms does not constitute an infringement [312] as defined by the Second Amendment.

        1.3.3.3.2. Pro:
        The definition of "arms [313]" used in the Constitution applies equally to modern firearms.

      1.3.3.4. Con:
      Gun technology has dramatically changed [314] since the Second Amendment was introduced. Fully automatic weapons today can fire up to 1000 [315] rounds per minute, whereas a gun in 1781 could only fire a single manually loaded shot at a time (pp. 3–4 [316]). As such, gun laws—in the context of the Constitution—are outdated.

        1.3.3.4.1. Pro:
        A great deal of modern military technology has a destructive capacity [317] that far exceeds the acceptable civilian uses of weaponry, such as sport or personal self-defense.

        1.3.3.4.2. Con:
        The right of civilians to make use of available weapon technology [318] to defend themselves against government oppression is not affected by advances in such technology.

      1.3.3.5. Pro:
      The Declaration of Independence states that it is the duty of citizens to throw off [319] any government which abuses its power or becomes overly oppressive.

        1.3.3.5.1. Pro:
        The US Revolutionary War relied heavily on soldiers bringing personal weapons [320] to use in the conflict.

        1.3.3.5.2. Pro:
        The US is built upon fundamental distrust of the government [321].

        1.3.3.5.3. Con:
        In Federalist 46 [322], Madison used the notion of the state militia to argue that the government should be designed in such a way that it would never be necessary to overthrow in the first place.

        1.3.3.5.4. Con:
        The right to form a militia in the US Constitution is often cited as justification for unjustified acts of terrorism and violence against government officials [323].

          1.3.3.5.4.1. Pro:
          Timothy McVeigh [324], one of the individuals convicted of bombing the Murrah Building in Oklahoma City [325] in 1995, plotted the attack with militant militia groups in an effort to defend the Second Amendment from the tyranny of the federal government.

          1.3.3.5.4.2. Con:
          Even if the right to form an armed militia is misused, it remains protected by the US Constitution (p. 886 [326]).

            1.3.3.5.4.2.1. Con:
            There is no constitutional right to organize a private armed militia [327].

          1.3.3.5.4.3. Pro:
          Anti-government organizations often make unfounded claims [328] that gun control legislation empowers totalitarianism and thus should be resisted with violence.

    1.3.4. Con:
    The US Constitution [329] is a living document [330], which means that it is subject to interpretation and can be amended [331].

      1.3.4.1. Pro:
      Amendments to the Constitution can be altered or removed by a new amendment; for example, the 21st Amendment was ratified in 1933 [332], repealing the 18th Amendment and thereby ending prohibition.

        1.3.4.1.1. Pro:
        A new amendment could establish specific definitions and limitations on the Second Amendment right to bear arms; some states already have proposed amendments [333] of this type.

        1.3.4.1.2. Con:
        A constitutional amendment could also further enshrine the right to bear arms and remove existing restrictions [334] on gun purchases and ownership; such amendments have already been proposed in some states.

      1.3.4.2. Con:
      Amending the US Constitution requires significant consensus [335] among the states. This may be impossible to achieve, given the politically contentious nature of gun control legislation.

        1.3.4.2.1. Pro:
        The only other method available for amending the US Constitution is via a Constitutional Convention [336], and this has never been attempted.

        1.3.4.2.2. Con:
        Increasing awareness of the link between weak laws and gun violence [337] can help lead to broader consensus among lawmakers.

      1.3.4.3. Con:
      Amendments to the US Constitution are often proposed, but rarely ratified; while over 11,000 have been proposed, only 27 amendments [338] have passed.

        1.3.4.3.1. Con:
        The epidemic of gun violence in the US is a social concern on par with the other issues that have led to constitutional amendments [339], such as slavery and the right to vote.

        1.3.4.3.2. Pro:
        Gun control does not rank among the most requested constitutional amendments [340] since 1999.

      1.3.4.4. Pro:
      Same-sex marriage was not federally recognized until 2015, when the US Supreme Court ruled in Obergefell v. Hodges [341] that everyone in the US has the constitutional right to marry.

        1.3.4.4.1. Con:
        The legal recognition of same-sex marriages represents an expansion of rights [342], as opposed to gun control, which represents a restriction of existing rights [343].

        1.3.4.4.2. Pro:
        Recognition of same-sex marriage was a comparably divisive issue in the US [344]; the gun control issue is similarly unlikely to be resolved by legislation and is likely to be defined by the Supreme Court.

    1.3.5. Pro:
    Due to the extent of this belief, the US exhibits a deepening partisan and ethnic divide [345] over the issue of gun ownership and legislation.

      1.3.5.1. Pro:
      Historically, gun control laws [346] have unjustly infringed upon the rights of marginalized communities of color by failing to protect their Second Amendment rights. Expanding or adding new gun control laws could lead to further discrimination.

        1.3.5.1.1. Pro:
        People of color are unfairly targeted by the enforcement of current gun control laws (p. 389 [347]).

          1.3.5.1.1.1. Pro:
          -> See 1.1.4.4.2.1.

          1.3.5.1.1.2. Pro:
          Black men are disproportionately judged to pose an imminent threat (p. 1 [348]), and are 2.8 times more likely to be killed by police than white men (p. 295 [349]).

          1.3.5.1.1.3. Con:
          While current gun law enforcement disenfranchises minority populations, gun control reforms could [350] be used to create new programs, universal gun control laws [351], and practices that address these inequalities.

            1.3.5.1.1.3.1. Pro:
            States that have enacted strict gun control reform have significantly reduced rates [352] of police officers fatally shooting civilians (p. 1070 [353]).

            1.3.5.1.1.3.2. Con:
            The implicit bias [354] which associates racial minorities with criminal activity is already widely exhibited by US law enforcement, thus any change in legislation is unlikely to alter this.

        1.3.5.1.2. Con:
        Many minority groups have availed themselves of the Second Amendment and own guns without being discriminated against [355].

        1.3.5.1.3. Pro:
        Current gun control legislation has its roots in racial discrimination [356], which disproportionately prevents minorities from owning firearms.

          1.3.5.1.3.1. Pro:
          While acting as the Governor of California, Ronald Reagan enacted gun control in response [357] to the Black Panthers openly carrying weapons into the Capitol.

          1.3.5.1.3.2. Con:
          While there is evidence of racial bias in the enforcement of many laws [358], that doesn't mean that the laws are written in a discriminatory way.

          1.3.5.1.3.3. Pro:
          The licensing process can be discriminatory [359] towards minority groups. In Illinois, only eight percent of licenses have been issued to African-Americans.

      1.3.5.2. Pro:
      Gun ownership is the strongest predictor of party affiliation [360] in the US.

        1.3.5.2.1. Con:
        The partisan positions on gun control in the US are philosophically at odds with the partisan positions on abortion (pp. 99–100 [361]).

        1.3.5.2.2. Pro:
        Nearly four times as many Republicans as Democrats believe that the right to own a gun supersedes [362] the need to protect citizens from gun-related violence.

      1.3.5.3. Con:
      Ethnic division over gun control is largely a result of fearmongering; the NRA has stoked fear of minorities [363] in order to scare people and make them more likely to want to own a gun and protest stricter gun regulations.

        1.3.5.3.1. Pro:
        The NRA uses racially charged language [364] to oppose gun control, echoing the rhetoric historically used to oppose minority rights in the US.

        1.3.5.3.2. Con:
        In the face of increasing gun ownership among minority groups, the NRA has started to adjust its ads and policies to be more inclusive [365].

      1.3.5.4. Con:
      The desire of a small segment of the population to own or use guns for leisure or protection is not imperative enough to justify not intervening against widespread criminal gun violence [366].

        1.3.5.4.1. Pro:
        Roughly one-third [367] of adults in the US claim to personally own a gun, demonstrating that at least the remaining nearly two-thirds would benefit from widespread intervention.

          1.3.5.4.1.1. Con:
          Roughly one-third [368] of the US population [369] equates to more than 100 million people, which is greater than the population of the largest central European country, Germany [370].

        1.3.5.4.2. Con:
        As of 2009, there were around twice as many guns per capita in the US as there were in 1968 (over 300 million additional firearms in all) (pp. 8–9 [371]).

  1.4. Con:
  Before enacting new, more effective gun control laws, the failures of current laws and regulations regarding firearms must be addressed.

    1.4.1. Con:
    The existing laws suffer from a number of notable gaps that can only be remedied via new legislation.

      1.4.1.1. Pro:
      Many of the existing firearm laws lack specificity.

        1.4.1.1.1. Pro:
        Current unspecific laws make it difficult to classify [372] a wide variety of firearms.

          1.4.1.1.1.1. Pro:
          Unclear laws have led to difficulties in classifying assault weapons [373], which are often misconstrued to comprise machine guns.

          1.4.1.1.1.2. Pro:
          New gun control legislation [374] can easily include accurate and comprehensive classification [375] to avoid the ambiguity of the current laws.

          1.4.1.1.1.3. Con:
          More specific classification of firearms is unnecessary, as the right to own and bear arms is unaffected by the type of weapon [376].

        1.4.1.1.2. Con:
        The average age of mass shooting perpetrators is over 30 years old, so any legislation establishing a minimum age to possess or purchase a firearm is unlikely to be effective at reducing the frequency of such attacks (p. 5 [377]).

          1.4.1.1.2.1. Pro:
          The vast majority of the under-30s who have committed mass school shootings stole guns [378] from friends or family. Raising the minimum age to purchase firearms will not reduce the frequency of these shootings.

            1.4.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
            Even if 80% of the guns in question were stolen, a 20% reduction in gun violence by those under 30 would represent significant progress, as this group is responsible for the bulk of all homicides in the US (p. 77 [379]).

          1.4.1.1.2.2. Con:
          Age requirements for licensing have been successfully used for regulating the use of cars [380], alcohol [381], and tobacco [382].

            1.4.1.1.2.2.1. Con:
            Alcohol and tobacco are highly unlikely to immediately kill someone; they are age-restricted due to addictiveness [383]. Cars have built-in safety features [384] to prevent as many deaths as possible during accidents.

            1.4.1.1.2.2.2. Pro:
            Half of all firearm homicides are committed by young people [385]; an age requirement would likely prevent many unnecessary deaths.

        1.4.1.1.3. Pro:
        Whether motorcycles are acceptable vehicles for transporting firearms [386] is a gray area in many states.

      1.4.1.2. Pro:
      While background checks are technically required by law when purchasing a firearm, loopholes [387] within the law can enable these checks to be easily bypassed.

        1.4.1.2.1. Pro:
        The COVID-19 pandemic contributed to a spike in gun sales in the US—many of which proceeded without background checks, demonstrating the inadequacy [388] of current gun control laws.

        1.4.1.2.2. Con:
        The existing gun control laws could easily be amended to limit the number of loopholes and exemptions.

      1.4.1.3. Con:
      With adequate enforcement, existing laws would be sufficient for reducing gun violence.

        1.4.1.3.1. Pro:
        It does not make sense to pass new laws when the current laws are not being enforced, since new laws are equally likely to go unenforced.

        1.4.1.3.2. Con:
        Enforcing existing regulations does not preclude strengthening gun regulations and adopting new universal gun laws; the two are not mutually exclusive.

      1.4.1.4. Con:
      New federal gun regulations are likely to clash with state laws and lead to protracted legal battles.

        1.4.1.4.1. Con:
        Federal regulations would be easier to enforce than state or city regulations because state and city regulations can be avoided by visiting an area with less strict laws.

        1.4.1.4.2. Pro:
        Enforcing federal standards could prove to be difficult where such laws conflict with state laws [389].

          1.4.1.4.2.1. Con:
          Per Article VI of the US Constitution, federal law preempts [390] state law.

    1.4.2. Pro:
    Gun control legislation remains a divisive issue among [391] US lawmakers, making it difficult to enact new laws.

      1.4.2.1. Con:
      There is evidence that the epidemic of gun violence in the US has led to increased consensus among Americans [392] that stricter controls are necessary.

        1.4.2.1.1. Pro:
        The proportion of Americans who believe that reducing gun violence is more important than gun rights is at its highest in over a decade [393].

        1.4.2.1.2. Con:
        The percentage of Americans who believe that more people carrying guns [394] will help to reduce gun violence has risen dramatically in recent years.

      1.4.2.2. Pro:
      Numerous gun control legislation proposals in the US have been rejected by lawmakers [395].

        1.4.2.2.1. Pro:
        Despite having majority support, many proposals since 2013 have failed to garner enough support [396] to become laws.

        1.4.2.2.2. Con:
        Gun control bills are no different from other laws in this regard; typically only 4–9% of proposed laws [397] are passed each year.

      1.4.2.3. Con:
      While federal laws have proved to be resistant to change, a number of states [398] have taken it upon themselves to enact stricter gun control legislation.

        1.4.2.3.1. Con:
        The variance of legislation from state to state and the confusion associated with poorly worded state laws be addressed by enacting strict federal gun control legislation (pp. 2–4 [399]).

          1.4.2.3.1.1. Pro:
          In Arkansas, carrying a firearm while on a "journey" is legal, but the definition of a journey includes any travel outside one's place of residence or county; this has been interpreted by gun rights advocates as support for the right to openly carry a firearm [400].

          1.4.2.3.1.2. Con:
          The preemption of local and state gun laws by federal regulations inhibits the ability of local governments to develop and implement targeted new strategies for gun control [401].

          1.4.2.3.1.3. Pro:
          The ability to carry a concealed firearm [402] is restricted in many states, and not all states allow reciprocity of that right.

        1.4.2.3.2. Pro:
        Given that the federal government is prevented from regulating guns by the Second Amendment [403], and that the powers not enumerated by the Constitution are reserved to the states by the Tenth Amendment [404], state legislatures are the correct venue for determining gun control laws (p. 1230 [405]).

      1.4.2.4. Pro:
      Any discussion of new gun control regulations in the US has resulted in large-scale protests [406] by gun rights activists [407].

        1.4.2.4.1. Con:
        In 2022, over 70,000 people attended the Washington, DC March For Our Lives [408] protest in favor of gun control, and there were over 400 sibling marches across the US.

        1.4.2.4.2. Pro:
        Given such displays of support, lawmakers in many jurisdictions would risk losing re-election [409] if they were to support stricter gun control policies.

    1.4.3. Pro:
    Attempting to implement new, stricter gun control measures is likely to spark backlash from US citizens and residents.

      1.4.3.1. Con:
      The possibility for backlash to occur is not a sufficient reason to abandon the implementation of a potentially life-saving [410] policy.

        1.4.3.1.1. Pro:
        Social progress and desegregation [411] during the civil rights movement came with the inherent risk of backlash, yet the benefits outweighed the potential harms.

        1.4.3.1.2. Con:
        Police concerns over backlash from citizens often result in reduced efforts at enforcement [412], which prevents such laws from having their intended effect.

      1.4.3.2. Con:
      Those who disagree with the implementation of legally enacted gun control laws are free to use their First Amendment [413] rights to assemble in peaceful protest and petition the government.

        1.4.3.2.1. Pro:
        Disagreeing with democratically passed legislation does not give one the right to act in a threatening or illegal manner [414].

        1.4.3.2.2. Con:
        Any measure that undermines the Second Amendment is a threat to the sovereignty of American citizens [415] and calls the legitimacy of the government into question.

      1.4.3.3. Pro:
      In the US, the fear [416] of gun control being enacted actually negates the aim of most gun control legislation, which is to reduce the number of available firearms.

        1.4.3.3.1. Pro:
        Gun sales spike [417] whenever stricter gun control is suggested by political leaders.

        1.4.3.3.2. Con:
        The actual implementation of laws restricting access to firearms, instead of perpetually discussing and balking at it, would resolve the main issue of people stockpiling weapons.

      1.4.3.4. Pro:
      There are roughly 1,000 anti-government extremist [418] groups in the US, and the vast majority of them consider gun rights to be paramount.

        1.4.3.4.1. Pro:
        Laws that target gun ownership risk alienating [419] people with conservative, right-wing [420] political views. This contributes to the continued emergence of violent extremists.

          1.4.3.4.1.1. Con:
          Right-wing extremist groups are declining in numbers [421].

        1.4.3.4.2. Pro:
        The narrative [422] of many right-wing militias is that the government is out to confiscate their weapons, which makes them especially sensitive to gun control legislation.

        1.4.3.4.3. Con:
        Government policy should be based on evidence [423] rather than on the perspectives and demands of radicalized populations.

        1.4.3.4.4. Con:
        Heavily armed right-wing and white nationalist [424] extremism has been responsible for many mass shootings [425] and other acts of domestic terrorism [426]; the growing presence [427] of these groups and the increased frequency of such events could be reduced or eliminated by new federal gun control regulations.

          1.4.3.4.4.1. Con:
          Targeting alt-right extremists by enacting sweeping gun regulations is less effective than deplatforming [428] them and restricting their ability to organize online [429].

          1.4.3.4.4.2. Con:
          In the face of anti-government terrorism [430], the US has demonstrated a willingness to hold massive federal investigations [431] and make targeted arrests. This suggests that better gun regulations may not be necessary for this purpose.

          1.4.3.4.4.3. Pro:
          Gun rights activism is used as a recruitment tool [432] by extremist groups.

    1.4.4. Con:
    New, more effective gun control laws have been implemented in other industrialized countries [433] and have resulted in fewer [434] gun-related homicides than the existing laws in the US.

      1.4.4.1. Pro:
      After two mass shootings in 2023, Serbia instituted stricter gun control laws [435] to prevent future ones from occurring.

        1.4.4.1.1. Con:
        The recency of such legislation makes it difficult to determine whether or not it has had any effect on improving safety in Serbia.

        1.4.4.1.2. Pro:
        Thousands of people voluntarily handed over their weapons, demonstrating public support [436] for the new laws.

      1.4.4.2. Pro:
      All guns are tightly controlled [437] and must be licensed in the UK, resulting in around six [438] guns per 100 citizens. The UK has only had one mass shooting [439] since the implementation of the laws in 1996.

        1.4.4.2.1. Con:
        UK gun control has not decreased violence [440], but changed the choice of weapon, which is essentially a failure of gun control as a method to reduce violence overall.

        1.4.4.2.2. Con:
        While Britain had a relatively low homicide rate before banning firearms in 1996, the murder rate in Britain increased after the ban and spiked [441] in 2002/2003 before returning to normal.

          1.4.4.2.2.1. Pro:
          In 2002, the UK experienced its highest homicide rate of the last 25 years, at 1.87 [442] deaths per 100,000 people.

            1.4.4.2.2.1.1. Con:
            The highest homicide rate in the last 25 years is evidence of general violence, not gun violence in particular.

          1.4.4.2.2.2. Con:
          Crime reporting standards in the UK were changed in 2002, so any data collected up to that point is incomparable with the data collected after the change (p. 46 [443]).

        1.4.4.2.3. Pro:
        Once the complicating factors had abated, gun crime in the UK [444] continuously declined following the implementation of the Firearms Act.

        1.4.4.2.4. Pro:
        Regulation [445] in Scotland following a mass shooting led to a three-quarters reduction [446] in firearm incidents.

      1.4.4.3. Con:
      Stricter firearm ownership laws will drive gun sales underground [447], which involves illegally making or modifying guns and selling them on the black market [448].

        1.4.4.3.1. Pro:
        Improvised zip guns [449] and those with 3D printed receivers [450] are easy to make [451] at home.

          1.4.4.3.1.1. Pro:
          While the Undetectable Firearms Act [452] prohibits the ownership of any firearms that cannot be detected by a metal detector, this restriction does not pertain to all 3D-printed guns [453], making them hard to control and regulate.

          1.4.4.3.1.2. Con:
          The plastics used in 3D printing are too brittle to make a functional gun, and metal-based 3D printers are too expensive to work as a meaningful alternative [454].

          1.4.4.3.1.3. Con:
          People can already buy 80% or lower receivers [455] without serial numbers.

        1.4.4.3.2. Con:
        The presence of guns in areas with strict gun control legislation can usually be traced back to gun sales in areas with lenient gun regulations [456] (p. 1291 [457]).

          1.4.4.3.2.1. Con:
          Katie Couric attempted to establish that gun laws can easily be circumvented in her documentary, "Under the Gun [458]," but was criticized [459] for editing footage to disparage gun advocates.

            1.4.4.3.2.1.1. Con:
            The criticisms [460] levied against "Under the Gun" only undermine Katie Couric's credibility as a producer and do not address the evidence being presented.

          1.4.4.3.2.2. Pro:
          Many US gun traders abuse weak gun laws in neighboring [461] states in order to traffic in firearms across state lines [462].

        1.4.4.3.3. Pro:
        The National Rifle Association (NRA) argues that firearms associated with criminal activity are linked to the black market [463], and thus should not be used as justification for gun regulations.

          1.4.4.3.3.1. Con:
          Despite evidence that criminals typically cannot acquire firearms within states with strict regulations, the NRA uses the idea of the black market [464] to campaign against gun control legislation.

          1.4.4.3.3.2. Pro:
          A 2016 US Department of Justice (DOJ) report indicated that over half (56%) of firearms used to commit crimes were either stolen, acquired at the scene of the crime, or purchased on the street or from the black market. Only 1.3% were purchased from a licensed dealer (p. 1 [465]).

          1.4.4.3.3.3. Pro:
          Approximately 43% of the guns used in US crimes [466] are purchased on the black market.

        1.4.4.3.4. Con:
        The black market for firearms in the US is already thriving, facilitated by weak gun control laws [467].

          1.4.4.3.4.1. Con:
          There is evidence that areas with stricter gun control laws have a larger black market [468] for guns.

          1.4.4.3.4.2. Pro:
          Firearm dealers in the US are rarely inspected or held accountable [469] for violations.

        1.4.4.3.5. Con:
        Selling a gun on the black market requires significant interaction with serious criminal elements.

          1.4.4.3.5.1. Con:
          Black market gun sales have increasingly moved to the dark web [470], where purchases can be made easily and anonymously.

          1.4.4.3.5.2. Pro:
          Illicit gun sales that take place in person come with additional risks, such as violent [471] robbery or assault.

        1.4.4.3.6. Pro:
        It is relatively easy to prepare a gun to be sold illegally. A gun can be made untraceable by using a power tool [472] to effectively grind away the serial number [473].

          1.4.4.3.6.1. Con:
          Scientists have developed a reagent that can restore [474] previously obliterated serial numbers from metal surfaces. This may be useful to law enforcement in tracing defaced firearms.

          1.4.4.3.6.2. Pro:
          A gun trace will only provide the history of a gun up to its last legal, registered owner [475].

      1.4.4.4. Pro:
      Gun control legislation implemented in Australia has been effective at minimizing the risk of mass shootings [476].

        1.4.4.4.1. Pro:
        Australia substantially reduced the number of firearms in public hands through buyback incentives [477].

          1.4.4.4.1.1. Pro:
          There is evidence that Australia's buyback program reduced the number of gun-related homicides and suicides [478].

          1.4.4.4.1.2. Con:
          Because of the large number [479] of firearms present in the US compared to Australia, it is unlikely that the Australian experience [480] with gun buybacks would be replicable in the US (p. 3 [481]).

          1.4.4.4.1.3. Con:
          The implementation of buyback schemes has not [482] been effective in Australia.

            1.4.4.4.1.3.1. Con:
            While the rate of gun crime has increased since laws were first strengthened in 1996, this more reflects the erosion of Australia's gun control regime [483], rather than inherent flaws in its original design.

            1.4.4.4.1.3.2. Pro:
            Australia [484] now has more firearms [485] in public possession than before the buyback.

        1.4.4.4.2. Con:
        Unlike the US Constitution, the Australian Constitution does not grant citizens the right to bear arms (p. 2 [486]).

      1.4.4.5. Con:
      Many countries with lower gun ownership rates and stricter gun control continue to experience high homicide rates. This undermines [487] the conclusion that gun control causes a reduction in gun-related murders.

        1.4.4.5.1. Pro:
        The Canadian government invested millions of dollars to adopt a strict gun licensing program [488] that had no significant effect on firearm homicides.

        1.4.4.5.2. Pro:
        Although the country has since relaxed its laws [489], following the enactment of massive gun possession restrictions, Brazil [490] had as few as eight legal firearms per 100 people, yet still ranked at the top for firearm-related homicides.

          1.4.4.5.2.1. Con:
          Following the legalization of guns in Brazil, gun-related violence spiked [491], suggesting that the correlation between gun ownership and gun violence is valid.

          1.4.4.5.2.2. Pro:
          The primary sources of illegal firearms in Brazil are police and military stockpiles [492], so stricter gun control laws are unlikely to be effective.

        1.4.4.5.3. Con:
        Despite having a high rate of gun ownership and cultural attachment to firearms [493], Switzerland's gun restrictions have resulted in a significantly lower gun homicide rate than that of the US.

          1.4.4.5.3.1. Pro:
